1968. INNES SCOTT was indicted for bigamy.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES WILLIAM BURRIDGE ( policeman, V 160). I produce two certificates of marriages—I have examined them by the register-books, and they are correct copies—I have known the prisoner's first wife for seven years—I saw her alive on 17th Oct., in Cornwall-road, Hammersmith. ( The certificate were here read.)
ELIZABETH STALKER . I am a widow, and live at Hammersmith. I was present in Clapham Church on 31st Dec, 1845, at the marriage of my daughter Caroline with the prisoner—this certificate refers to my daughter's marriage—she is now living, and has been living with me the last three years—she is twenty-eight years of age.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. When she was married, did you know that the prisoner was only nineteen? A. No, not till afterwards—she was compelled to leave him, and has been absent from him three years—during that time there has not been the slightest communication between them—she has never beheld him—I have been living at Hammersmith about three years, since I left the Wandsworth-road—I live in Cornwall-road, Hammersmith) about ten minutes' walk from the Church—I occasionally frequent that Church with others, not constantly—I attend with my daughter occasionally—the prisoner and my daughter had no family—there was never the slightest approach to a family; I am certain about that—my daughter got the license—I did not accompany her to get it—the prisoner was at Manchester at the time—it was got by his desire.
COURT. Q. Were you a widow at the time of the first marriage?
A. Yes, and my daughter was living with me—I was not in business—the prisoner had a situation, or was on the point of having one, in the Admiralty.
TEMPERANCE CAROLINE BAKER . I live in Acton-place, Manor-street, Clapham. On 6th Feb. last I was married to the prisoner in Hammersmith Church—I had known him a few months previously—I had been married before—I accused the prisoner, six weeks before my marriage to him, before Elizabeth York, who had told me he had been married, and he said, "My wife has been dead three years; she died in childbed at Brussels"
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you said, "Are you married?" A. I said, "Innes, my friend has told me that you are married;" and he said, before her, "My wife has been dead three years; she died in childbed at Brussels"—the prisoner was living in my neighbourhood, just below me, and his family—I think he had a situation in the Admiralty—I keep a beer-shop—I know the prisoner's family, but I had no intercourse with them, only by seeing them at Church—he had sisters and brothers—they were all living in the neighbourhood except one, who was living at Tewkesbury—we were married at Hammersmith Church; that was by mutual agreement—I afterwards heard the prisoner was married, and I felt it my duty to institute this proceeding—I went to his first wife's mother on the 16th of this month—the fact of his previous marriage was not talked of before our marriage, only by my friend—after our marriage it was not talked of more than once, to my knowledge; that was in presence of my servant—it appears that every one. in the neighbourhood knew it but myself—I am not in the habit of going from home—I had not heard it.
Q. Did he not tell you that he had heard his wife was dead, that he had had information on which he could rely, that his wife was dead, from a person of the name of Waddilove? A. He said she was dead; Waddilove's name was never mentioned till after the marriage—he said decidedly his wife was dead.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. And on that you married him? A. Yes; his brother's were in the habit of visiting at my house.
COURT. Q. What was your first husband? A. A licensed-victualler—I keep the same house that he died in four years ago—I have what this brings me in, and some leasehold houses which are settled on me by will—I shall be thirty-nine years of age in December.
ELIZABETH YORK . I live in Cross-street, Clapham, and am unmarried. I know the last witness—I made a communication to her about the prisoner, and she said to him, "I hear you have another wife," or "have got a wife," or something to that effect; and he said, "I bad a wife; she has been dead three years or three years and a half; she died in Brussels, in child-birth."
---- SALTER ( police-sergeant, V 15). I apprehended the prisoner at Mrs. Baker's, in Marion-street, Clapham—I have known him many years.
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutrix. — Confined Six Months .
[trialAccount t18511027-1969]
1969. DANIEL CLANCEY , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Williams and another, and stealing 2 tablecloths, and other articles, value 21 s.; the goods of Thomas Williams: and other articles, value 2 l. 15 s.; the goods of Charles Rimmer.
MR. CARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
MARIA CONLEY . I live at 68, Blackfriars-road, and am cook and house-keeper to Mr. Thomas Williams, and Charles Rimmer, woollen-drapers. Mr.
Williams sleeps in the house—Mr. Rimmer does not, but he lives on Sunday morning, 28th Sept., I got up at 7 o'clock, I was the first person up—I went to the second-floor back-room—I found the door open—it had been open the night before—I had been the last person up the night before—I went to bed about half-past eleven—there was a chest in that room which had contained linen—I saw it about half-past ten on the Saturday-night—it was shut, but not locked—it contained night-shirts, day-shirts, and other things, and two tablecloths—at seven next morning the chest was open, and the things were turned over; I examined and found that two tablecloths were gone—there is a cupboard in that room—the door of it is shut, but not locked—in the morning it was open, and a carpet-bag which had hung up in—that cupboard had been taken down, and thrown down by the clothes—I had seen the contents of the cupboard safe on the Saturday-night—I missed some boots from that cupboard—I could not at that time tell how many, but here were three pairs missing—I have seen one pair since, they belong to Mr. Rimmer—a pair of pumps were also missing—there was a chest of drawers in that room—in the morning one of the drawers was on the floor, and empty—another drawer was open, and the things in it turned over—four or five waistcoats were missing, which had been in that drawer—I think some of them were silk—I had seen them on Saturday; I had opened that drawer to put things away—there were three flannel-shirts missing from that drawer, and a pair of flannel-drawers, and a new silk handkerchief—the waistcoats belonged to Mr. Williams, and the other things to Mr. Rimmer—I had teen them safe on the Saturday night, when I put away the clothes—I afterwards went down stairs, and found the middle window open in the passage, which is between the first and the second-floor—the passage goes along the staircase, and there are three windows in it which open on the leads, which go over the circus in the shop, and right back to Collingwood-street; from those leads you could get to Collingwood-street very easily—I cannot say how high they are from the ground—I had seen those windows on the preceding evening the last thing before I went to bed—they were all three snapped—they lift up and fasten with a catch or snap in the middle—the pane under the snap in the middle window had been broken a few days before—any person, by putting their arm through that broken pane, could unfasten the snap very easily—it was that middle window that was open—that passage leads to ill parts of the house.
EDWARD LANE . I am servant to Mr. Bellworthy, a butcher, who lives in Great Charlotte-street. His slaughter-house is in Collingwood-street, close at the corner, at the back of my master's house—on Sunday, 28th Sept., between 6 and 7 o'clock in the morning, I found two pair of boots, a leg of mutton, and a small crow-bar, on the tiles of my master's kitchen, which ii over his slaughter-house—any one could easily get on those tiles from the ground in Collingwood-street—there is a window with iron bars across it instead of glass—they could nearly reach the tiles from the ground, and from the tiles of the kitchen a person could go across to the roof to Mr. Williams'—I brought the things down to my master, and got a policeman—the things were given to him.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Where did you find the leg of mutton? A. On the tiles—I do not know how it came there—I think I mentioned about the leg of mutton once before—the prosecutor lives about a stone's throw from my master's—you pass over six or seven houses to it—you can get to any other house as well.
JAMES FROST ( policeman, L 59). On Sunday morning, 28th Sept., I was
on duty in Black friars-road, in private clothes, about half-past 8 o'clock—I saw the prisoner in company with two others, coining in a direction from Collingwood-street, across Charlotte-street, to Blackfriars-road—when I first saw them they might be five or six yards from the corner of Collingwood-street—Mr. Bellworthy's shop is at the corner of Collingwood-street—I observed one of the persons who was with the prisoner looked rather bulky about the body, as if be had things about him—I followed them up Blackfriars-road to the corner of Wellington-street—I had a view of all three of their faces, I am sure the prisoner was one—that was 300 or 400 yards from where I first saw them—I made a grasp at the prisoner, and the one who was bulky, whom I afterwards took, and he was discharged—I received a kick from the third man; and as I received the kick, the prisoner tripped me up—he catched me across the shinbone of the left leg—both my legs went from under me, and I was thrown down—I was stunned for a minute—as I fell I caught the coat of one of them, and took out a piece of the lining in my hand—that was the coat of the one that was bulky—as I fell I was kicked, and I was struck on the side of my head, I could not see by whom—they all three got away from me; at I recovered myself I jumped up—two of them ran down Wellington-street, and one down Blackfriars-road—when I got up I found a hat, and this table-cloth in it, about a yard and a half from where the scuffle took place—this is the hat and tablecloth—I had known the prisoner for a number of years—I met him afterwards on the Thursday, 2nd Oct., at the corner of Upper Marsh, Waterloo-road—he was alone—I took him into custody—I told him it was for a robbery; and as he was going to the station, lie said, "Is it concerning Allen?" (who was then in custody)—I said, "Yes"—he said he was an innocent man; be bad read it in the paper, but he knew nothing about it—I had not said anything about it before, too further than telling him I wanted him for felony—Patrick Allen's name had not been mentioned—I took the prisoner to the station—I afterwards examined Mr. Bellworthy's slaughter-house—I found some marks down the side of the wall, and some footmarks leading from the slaughter-house to Mr. Williams'.
Cross-examined. Q. Where is Collingwood-street? A. It is the first turning in Charlotte-street, on the right-hand side—I saw the party coming in a direction from Collingwood-street'—I do not know which of the three this bat belonged to—I did not see which it was lost their hat—I have before said that I was struck when I was falling; I cannot tell by whom—I could not say that the prisoner did more to me than tripped me on the left leg; I am positive that was the prisoner—I took Allen into custody; he was the man who appeared bulky—I took him on the Monday, and he was discharged—I did not see the prisoner till the Thursday evening following—to the best of my knowledge the prisoner had a cap on on the Sunday morning when I saw the three together—when I took him I told him it was for felony; and in going along he said, if it was with Allen he had read it in the paper, but he was innocent—when at the station, I told him it was a burglary in Blackfriars-road; he said he was innocent—I was before the Magistrate three times—Allen was discharged on the second examination.
MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Where did you take Allen? A. In Kennington-lane, when he was coming up to see his brother.
DANIEL MAHER . ( police-sergeant, L 38). On Sunday morning, 28th Sept., I was on duty in Blackfriars-road, at half-post 3 o'clock in the morning. I saw the prisoner with two others—the prisoner was about two steps before the other two—I first saw them on the pavement in front of Collingwood-street, in Great Charlotte-street—they crossed, and came to the corner of
Great Charlotte-street—Frost was in company with me; he was in plain clothes; I was in uniform—I followed the prisoner and the others with Frost—Frost followed them first, and I followed after Frost, to the corner of Wellington-street—Frost was running after the prisoner—the other two then crossed the road, and one stepped before Frost on the pavement, hit Frost on the head, and knocked him down.
COURT. Q. Had the prisoner crossed the road? A. Yes; Frost followed the prisoner; the other two then crossed the road, and one of the two got before Frost—Frost seized him, and he knocked Frost down with a blow on the side of the head, as well as I could see—Frost fell, and the man that struck him fell down with him—that was not the prisoner—I sprung my rattle, and ran to Frost's assistance—the man who struck Frost jumped up, and ran away directly—the other men ran up Black friars-road—I saw a hat lying near Frost—I did not see it picked up—the prisoner had a cap on—I have known him the last three months; I have seen him several times—I have no doubt he is one of the men I saw at that time—I could not take him—he took the first turning in Wellington-street, and ran towards St. George's Church—we had a long run for it—I could not catch him—the next time I saw him was on Thursday, 2nd Oct., at the station—he spoke to me first—he said, "You know me very well; you saw me at the coffee-shop"—he did not say what coffee-shop—he said it was on Saturday night—I had seen him at half-past 1 o'clock on Sunday morning, at the Albert coffee-shop, in Great Charlotte-street—I am sure I saw him there.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the bat fall from the party it belonged to? A. No, I did not—it was not the prisoner's hat; he had a cap on when I first saw him, and he had when I was running after him—I did not see him strike or kick Frost—I have never said that I doubted whether the prisoner was one of the party—I was examined before the Magistrate against this prisoner and Allen—when I saw the prisoner at the station he said I saw him in the coffee-shop—he denied the robbery—he said he knew nothing about the robbery, and had nothing to do with it—I do not think he said he was at home at the time—I cannot swear it.
FRANCIS IRELAND ( policeman, L 119). I was on duty, in Great Charlotte-street, on Sunday morning, 28th Sept. I went up to the door of a coffee-house, which I believe is called the Albert—it is kept by a man of the name of Sevenoaks—it was about half-past 1 o'clock—I saw the prisoner sitting down with two others in the coffee-shop—I afterwards saw the prisoner about a quarter-past 2, in Great Charlotte-street; he came from the opposite side towards Collingwood-street; he looked me very strictly in the face, and I took very particular notice of him, by seeing him previously in the coffee-shop—I was in uniform—I was about a yard from him—he walked down Collingwood-street, of him—I had known him about twelve months before—I am positive he is the man I saw.
Cross-examined. Q. Who took Allen? A. I believe it was Frost—I cannot say whether he was one of the men that were in the coffee-shop—I do not know which was the man that was bulky—I did not see them together.
Louis SEVENOAKS. I keep the Albert coffee-shop in Great Charlotte-street. I have seen the prisoner, and know him by sight—I remember Sunday morning, 28th Sept., the prisoner was in my coffee-shop about half-past 1 o'clock—he came a little before that—I saw the policeman, Maher, come in—the prisoner was in the coffee-shop when Maher came in.
Cross-examined. Q. Do the policemen use your shop? A. No; but I had
had a row on Saturday evening, and they were ordered to come in—I saw the prisoner come in with two men—Mr. Frost came and spoke to me, I think, a day or two afterwards—I think the prisoner had had one hearing—I did not take particular notice; I know it was some time after—I think it was after the first hearing; I am almost sure it was—Mr. Frost came and spoke to me about it, and said I was to go to identify the prisoner—I know I saw the prisoner in my house that night.
MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Has the prisoner been in the habit of visiting your coffee-house? A. I have seen him in a few times, perhaps five or six times, generally with some one else; he was with two more that night—I did not know either of them.
( The prisoner's statement before the Magistrate was here read: "I am not guilty of it; I was not there; I was not in the coffee-shop; I was in bed; I never saw the coffee-shop keeper before yesterday.")
JAMES FROST re-examined. This is the tablecloth I found in the hat.
MARIA CONLEY re-examined. I know this tablecloth, from my using it the last sixteen months—there is no mark on it—here is the fellow one to it ( producing it,) only one is longer than the other—I can swear this is my employer's tablecloth—it has been in daily use, with this other one, and two more—they are the same patterns, and the same person made the two, the porter's wife, who was there before I was—they are hemmed in the same manner—two tablecloths were taken away—the other one is not found—these boots belong to Mr. Rimmer—I know them, from so frequently taking them out of the cupboard.
Cross-examine. Q. Is Mr. Rimmer here? Q. No; those gentlemen are both single—I have never had a mistress—I did not see the porter's wife make these tablecloths—there were four tablecloths in use—I did not wash them—I laid them occasionally on the table for sixteen months—there is no mark on them.
MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Did you give them out to wash? A. Yes, and received them, and examined them, when they were brought back—besides that, I laid the cloth, and have such knowledge of them that I can swear to them. Witness for the Defence.
MARGARET GILL . I live at 3, Abbey-place, Smith-street, Hercules-buildings; my husband is a slater. I know the prisoner—he, and his father and mother, occupy an apartment in my house—I remember the policeman coming to our house to search, on the Thursday evening on which the prisoner was taken—I sleep in the lower part of the house, on the ground-floor—it is but a two roomed house—I recollect perfectly well the Saturday night before that Thursday—when the prisoner came home that night we were having our supper—my husband called him, and gave him a glass of porter—it did not exceed 20 minutes after 12 o'clock when he came in—I let him in, and fastened the door afterwards—I crossed from the public-house with a pot of beer when the clock struck twelve, and it was about twenty minutes afterwards when he came in—I saw him go up-stairs—I handed him the candle, and fastened the door after him—the door fastens with a bolt at top and bottom—I bolted them both, and there is a spring-lock—the lock could not be opened outside without a key, but a person outside could not get in with a key, because the door was bolted—the prisoner did not come down again, because he could not get out—I saw him again on Sunday morning—he came down between 9 and 10, in his stocking-feet and his shirt-sleeves—he brought a piece of bacon on a gridiron, and asked me to cook it for him, and I did—he could not get out of the house without my hearing him—I do not sleep
very soundly, because I do not enjoy the best of health—a person could not come down and undo the bolts of the door without my hearing them; and if he got out, he could not get in again, because I have no street-door key—I am able to tell that the prisoner was in the house from 20 minutes past 12 till I saw him in the morning—the door was fastened in the morning as I had left it.
Cross-examined by MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Who lives in the house? A. No one but his father and mother, and brother, and a young woman, named Flaharty, who is now in her confinement, who attends his mother, who is afflicted—the mother and the young woman sleep on the floor—the father and the two brothers sleep in the room—I am no relation to any of them—my husband is a labouring man—he works for Mr. North—he was at home that night—he saw the prisoner, and gave him a glass of porter—he saw him go out of the room—I got up from the table and gave him the candlestick—my husband was sitting at the table—when the prisoner received the candlestick he wished us good night, and went up stairs—I got up and shut the door when he went out—I did not go in their room, but J am well aware that his father and mother, Flaharty and himself, and his brother were there—Flaharty is married—her husband is a seafaring man—half-past 12 o'clock is not a late hour for me to be up on Saturday night—I have many little odds and ends to do—on Saturday night I am very often up till 1—on Monday morning my husband goes to work at 6—we sometimes go to bed at 10, and sometimes at 11—we never go to bed leaving the street-door open—we shut it—if I do not bolt it, it has a spring-lock—I remember the night the policeman came to our house—he knocked at the door, and then shoved the door, and I got up—the policeman did not enter the house without my knowledge he knocked and shoved the door—the door did open when he shoved against it, and I momentarily got up—he had knocked at my bed-room door before I got up—he was inside the street-door before I opened my bed-room door—to the best of my knowledge that was between 10 and 11—the prisoner has been in my place going on for three months—my husband is not here, he could not leave his situation to be here—the prisoner's father and his younger brother are here.
Q. How do you know that this was on 28th Sept.? A. I do not exactly know the day of the month—I heard the policeman say it was 28th Sept.—it was the Saturday before they came to search my house—the prisoner was mostly in the habit of coming into my room—he used to come down for a light or a pipe of tobacco.
MR. CHARNOCH. Q. Your husband is at work? A. Yes, as a slater, for Mr. North, in the Palace, New-road—it was on a Thursday night the policeman came to my house—he knocked at my door, and shoved the door against my parlour-door—he must have shoved with great force to get it open—I did not prevent his searching my place, but he did not offer to search my apartment—Ellen Flaherty is in her confinement.
COURT. Q. On the night the policeman came to search your house, was your husband at home? A. Yes, and my two children—my outer door was not bolted then—it is not when I am not up late, because sometimes they are working later than we are—on that occasion, I went to bed and left my door unbolted, because I have no street-door-key to accommodate them with—on that Sunday morning, I saw the prisoner between 9 and 10 o'clock, in his stockings—I was up that morning about 7—my husband is at his work to-day—he has worked for Mr. North regularly for eighteen or nineteen years—he knows that I am come here for this purpose—the prisoner bad
been lodging regularly at my house ever since they have been in my place, which is close upon three months—my house is a good step from Great Charlotte-street, Black friars—South-street, Hercules-buildings, is in Lambeth—I suppose it would take a quarter of an hour to walk from my place to Great Charlotte-street—the prisoner is what they call a hammer-man—since he has been with me, he has been working for some master in Webber-street, but I do not know his name.
MR. CHARNCCK proposed to call the prisoner's father.
Prisoner. I do not wish my father to be asked anything; he it generally drunk on Saturday nights, and he is so deaf he cannot hear.
COURT to Louis SEVENOAKS. Q. How long was the prisoner in your shop that night? A. Perhaps half an hour or three-quarters of an hour—I was attending the shop, and he was there all that time—I keep open all night.
( The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18511027-1970]
1970. MICHAEL RYAN , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Edward Newland, and stealing 1 shawl and 1 night-gown, value 35; the goods of Catharine Connelly.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
CATHARINE CONNELLY . I am servant to Mr. Edward Newland, at 5, Yorkterrace, Trinity Church-row, St. Mary Newington. In the night of Wednesday, 8th Oct., I was sleeping in the front-kitchen—I was awoke about half-past 2 o'clock in the morning—I saw two men in the room—I am quite sure the prisoner was one—he had a candle in his hand—I laid quite quiet, and took no notice of them—I watched them for a quarter of an hour—the prisoner went two or three times to my master's bureau, and put his fingers to try if it could be opened—it lifts up—while I was appearing to be asleep the prisoner came with the candle, and held it over my face—he did that several times, and I took the opportunity of looking at him—I saw him quite plainly—I looked at him often—during that time the other man was at the cupboard-door—he had nothing in his hand that I could see—he then turned to my master's box, and took a long thing from under his coat, and broke it open—I screamed out—the prisoner threw the candle at me; it fell on my dress—the prisoner and the other man went out of the kitchen; they stopped in the back-kitchen a little while—I heard my master's voice asking me what was the matter—the prisoner and the other man got away—my master came down, and on looking at the house, I found the back-kitchen window was open—I am quite sure it was closed when I went to bed about 12—I had fastened it with a catch—the box of the lock of the kitchen-door was unscrewed in the morning, and the door was open—the box was not unscrewed the night before—I locked the door myself—in the garden I found a shawl and a night-dress of mine—I had left them in the back-kitchen when I went to bed—the prisoner had a brown coat, corduroy trowsers, and a cap—he had whiskers under his chin, the same as he has now—I am quite sure he is the man who held the candle.
EDWARD NEWLAND . I am a City Missionary. I was awoke early in the morning on 8th Oct. by the scream of my servant—I learnt there were two men in her room—I gave an alarm to the police—they came, and I went down, and found the house broken open—a bundle was found in the garden, and different articles were strewed about the room.
THOMAS RICHARDS ( police sergeant M, 10). On the morning after this robbery I received information from Connelly, and by her description of the
prisoner's dress and person I apprehended him in Mint-street, the same afternoon—I told him I wanted him to go to the station with me—he said, "You always know where to find me"—Connelly was taken to the station, and I placed her and another female in the inspector's room—shortly after I left, the prisoner walked into that room by himself—directly Connelly saw him she screamed out, "That is the man that held the light over my face last night"—I afterwards saw the impression of a man's shoes in the prosecutor's garden and several other grounds—I took the shoes from the prisoner's feet at the station, and compared them with the impression—I put them in the impression, and they fitted—here is a particular heel on this one.—( The prisoner's statement before the Magistrate teas here ready as follows: "The prisoner says, 'I am innocent of the charge; I was at home on Tuesday night, and did not go out till half-past 8 next morning; I have a witness in Court to prove it.")
Prisoner's Defence. I declare to my God I am innocent of this charge. I was living in a policeman's house at this time; I told them where I lived; I have a witness to prove I was in bed.
ANN MARTIN . I live at Gravesend, and am a travelling woman—I came to town on Monday, 6th Oct., and stopped at the prisoner's mother's house—I saw the prisoner go to bed—I spent the evening and the night with his mother, who is my sister—she lives at No. 2, Warren-place, Dover-road—she is a widow—I heard no disturbance that night—I saw the prisoner in bed early the next morning—I went back on the Tuesday.
COURT to EDWARD NEWLAND. Q. On what day was your house robbed? A. On Wednesday morning, 8th Oct.
GUILTY . Aged 23.
The prisoner was further charged with having been before convicted: to which he pleaded
GUILTY.— Transported for Ten Years .
( The COURT directed that Catherine Connelly should receive a reward of 20 s).

Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18511027-1973]
1973. SPENCER ANSELL , embezzling 3 l. 4 s., 1 l., and 7 d.; the moneys of Charles Francis and others, his masters: to which he pleaded
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18511027-1974]
1974. SPENCER ANSELL , was again indicted for embezzlement.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
ALFRED FRANCIS . I am in partnership with Charles Francis and another—we are cement-manufacturers at Nine Elms, and carry on business in Great Scotland-yard—the prisoner was employed to manage our business at Great Scotland-yard—we furnished him with cement for the purpose of sale at that place—he had permission to sell the articles on cash and on credit—I myself
called there to see how business was going on—when he sold goods he was to enter them in his day-book—he was in the habit of furnishing his stock-paper weekly to our counting-house—I have here his stock-paper of 11th Sept; it contains the account of goods, showing the stock on hand of the preceding week—in addition to that, the stock he had received since from us—he charged himself with them, and he discharged himself by cash credit and expenses—it was his duty to pay over the balance to our cashier, George Judd—this is the stock-paper of 11th Sept.—it is the prisoner's writing—I took it from him—it is not finished—his expenses are not added to it—it would appear in the day-book to whom he had sold goods.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Had you anything in the world to do with this man yourself, either by employing him, or receiving the accounts from him, or receiving money from him, or giving him direction? A. I employed him, as one of the partners—he was in our employ some years as a labouring man—I did not pay him his wages—I took this stock-paper from him before it was completed—I was not in the habit of receiving his papers, or receiving money or accounts from him; that was all done through out cashier—the prisoner was employed at Great Scotland-yard, and he was employed at Nine Elms before we put him there—he was employed the last two or three years at Scotland-yard—he had to come to Nine Elms to give in his accounts—his wages were 20 s. a week—he had been with us probably six or seven years—we have filled up his place since he left, and we are giving higher wages.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. In what respect is this paper not complete? A. I think he has not charged his expenses—he has mentioned here the amount of goods sold—he was transcribing it from the slate and the day-book when I entered the warehouse.
GEORGE KEEF . I am a builder, and live in King-street, Soho. I deal with Messrs. Francis—I obtained goods from the prisoner at Scotland-yard—on 4th June I paid him 1 l. 10 s.; he gave me this receipt.
Cross-examined. Q. This is, I see, a bill made out in the name of Ansell; did you know about Francis' being in the business? A. I knew they were in the business—I got the things from the prisoner—I never saw anything of Francis in the matter—I had dealt with the prisoner for some time before this—this was a payment in part of the account—there was 2 s. 5 1/2 d. left to pay to make it all right—I have paid another account since—I suppose this was added to the other.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Is not the name "Francis" over the door, in Scotland-yard? A. Yes; I dealt at the yard previous to the prisoner being there—I paid the money with the intention of paying it into the firm, on account of the goods I had had from the yard.
WILLIAM LAYCOCK . I am a builder, and live in Hill-street, Berkley-Square; I have dealt with Messrs. Francis for cement On 23rd July I sent. Fitzgerald for some cement—I gave him 1 l.; there were two empty casks to be returned—he brought back the goods—I sent him for 9 s. 6 d. change, and a receipt for the money—this is it; the amount of the cement is 17 s. 6 d.; here is an allowance for the casks, and 10 s. 6 d. paid in money—he brought me 9 s. 6 d., and this bill.
THOMAS FITZGERALD . I am in Mr. Laycock's service—in July I got 1 l. from him—I gave it to some one in the office, I really cannot tell who, as it is some time ago—I received from the person this receipt and the change—I cannot tell how much change I got—I saw the mail write this receipt—I had been to that place once before.
ALFRED FRANCIS re-examined. This bill is the prisoners' writing—it is "Paid, S. Ansell."
ELIZABETH BEACH . My husband is a builder. He deals with Messrs. Francis for cement—I know the prisoner as warehouseman at Scotland-yard. On 11th Sept. I paid him 7 s. 6 d.—I got this receipt from him for 7 s. 7 1/2 d.
GEORGE WILLIAM JUDD . I am cashier to Messrs. Francis. I have been there twenty-six years—the prisoner was warehouseman in my masters' employ—it was his duty to sell the goods of Messrs. Francis in the yard—he had a check-book, and when he sold goods it was his duty to make out a check as a bill and receipt if the goods were sold for cash, and give that to the party who bought the goods; and if they were sold on credit, the goods were merely filled in—the counterfoil remained attached to the book—it was his duty to make out a weekly stock-paper—it consisted of the goods that had been sold during the week from the Friday to the Thursday night—he was to enter in the day-book the money and goods sold on credit—he brought the stock-paper and books to me every week—I used to look at the account and receive the money from him—the amount of cash in the stock-paper I compared with the amount in his day-book, and if they agreed I took the balance from him—he paid me at the time—the day-book was the voucher to me for the moneys that had been received by him—I considered that every. thing ought to be entered in the day-book—I looked at the day-book to see what had been received by him—I have the day-book here—I have compared it with the stock-papers from Jan. to Sept.—I have found that the stock-papers agree with the day-book—I do not find any entry in the day-book of 1 l. 10 s. received from Mr. Keith, or 10 s. 6 d. from Laycock, or 7 s. 7 1/2 d. from Beach.
Cross-examined. Q. How often was this book brought to you? A. Every Friday night, almost invariably—it has been on Saturday—I do not recollect that it ever went two weeks—the money received is, on both sides of this book, made up a total at the end of the week—it was by this book and the stock-paper he paid me the money—the book and the stock-papers agree as to the amount—he gave me the stock-paper and what appeared there—he occasionally left the book with me—from 23rd May till 11th June I was absent—Mr. Friend was there then—the prisoner has come sometimes on Friday evening and sometimes on Saturday—he has come when I was not there—in one or two instances I think he left the book with the wharf-clerk; but when I had the book the paper was in it, and I found the book and paper to agree—he might have left it with one of my fellow-clerks, but I do not recollect such an instance—this book is in his handwriting—there were no other clerks at Scotland-yard who might have made entries while he was there—there was no one but himself—if a large quantity of cement were delivered he would get assistance.
JURY. Q. How often did you examine the counterfoils and the stock-paper with the day-book? A. I examined the cash at the time—it was no part of my duty to examine the counterfoils with the book—that was done by a clerk at Nine Elms—he is not here; the book is.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. When he gave these bills to the customers, was it his duty to enter this money in the day-book? A. Yes; I was sometimes out when he came with money—I compared the day-book and stock-paper with the balance of money that had been left—I did that in all cases, and found it correct—I received from him, during the period I have mentioned, the sums that appeared to be entered in the book—the whole of the money entered in this book, and of course in the stock-papers, I received, and found it correct—I
did not in these accounts receive this 1 s. 10 s., or 10 s. 6 d., or 7 s. 7 1/2 d.—if in the way of his duty he had entered them as he ought to have done, either I or Mr. Friend would have received them.
JOHN EWEN FRIEND . I have been in the prosecutors' employ fourteen or fifteen years. From 23rd May to 11th June it was my duty to receive the day-book, the stock-papers, and cash from the prisoner—I found the day-book and stock-papers were correct as far as was stated—here is no entry of a payment of 1 l. 10 s. by Keef, on 4th June.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not know of Mr. Keef's existence? A. No; all the sums are put down with a name—if a man went there and bought goods without a name, it would be the prisoner's duty to ask his name—it is the custom of our trade to have the name of everybody, because we want to follow up the trade if we can—I never recollect seeing an entry without a name.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Do you know the prisoner's writing? A. Yes; these receipts of Mr. Beach, Mr. Laycock, and Mr. Keef are his writing—I have no doubt.
THOMAS CAPON . I took stock at Scotland-yard on 11th Sept I found a deficiency in the stock of 989 bushels of Roman cement, 48 cwt. of plaster, 49 bushels of Parian cement, and 1,808 lbs. of mastic.
JURY to MR. JUDD. Q. Have you the counterfoil-book here? A. Yes; this is the prisoner's handwriting—here are a number of leaves deficient—here is no counterfoil of Laycock's.
( The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, NOVEMBER 24 TH.
